Below is the text of the document I provided to law enforcement that day: *EXPLANATION FOR LAW ENFORCEMENT REGARDING FIVE-DAY NOTICE* *To Whom It May Concern,* *This document is intended to clarify the legal reasons why the five-day notice posted by law enforcement does not justify forcing us to leave the property without proper adherence to the legal process.* *Under Florida law, we qualify as squatters on private property, which makes this a civil matter, not a criminal one.

DEFINITION OF SQUATTING** *Florida law recognizes squatting as occupying a property without the owner’s explicit permission.

While we do not claim ownership of the property, our presence qualifies as squatting under Florida law.* *Florida Statute §82.01(1):* *‘Unlawful entry and forcible detainer’ mean entering into and taking possession of real property with force, stealth, or without the consent of the owner.* *Key Point: Squatting does not constitute trespassing once possession has been established through physical occupation, such as erecting a tent.

This means that the property owner cannot rely on law enforcement to force us to leave without going through the courts.* *Florida Statute §82.03:* *A person entitled to possession of real property has a cause of action against another who obtained possession wrongfully or forcibly.* *Florida Statute §83.21:* *The landlord may recover possession of rented premises only by an action for possession under s. 83.59 or other civil action.* *Key Point: The property owner must file for an unlawful detainer in court.

If the court grants the property owner a writ of possession, it will then be executed by law enforcement after the proper legal process is complete.* **3.

POLICE AUTHORITY IN CIVIL MATTERS** *Law enforcement does not have the authority to bypass the legal eviction process by issuing a five-day notice.* *Florida Statute §82.045(1):* *A person entitled to possession of real property may recover possession of the property by ejectment or by other legal action.* *Florida Statute §82.045(2):* *Law enforcement officers may not remove a person from the property unless a writ of possession has been issued by the court.* *Key Point: Until a valid writ of possession is issued by a court and served, law enforcement cannot legally remove us from the property.* **4.

MISREPRESENTATION OF THE FIVE-DAY NOTICE** *The five-day notice posted by the police holds no legal weight under Florida law.

It is not a court-issued writ of possession.* *Florida Statute §82.045(4):* *A property owner or authorized agent must file a civil action for removal or eviction before law enforcement can act.* *Key Point: Any attempt by law enforcement to force us to leave without a writ of possession is a violation of our Constitutional rights.* **5.

HISTORY OF EVICTION COMPLIANCE** *We are already fully aware of the legal eviction process in Miami-Dade county, having gone through it previously.* *In Wireless Boutique, Inc. v.

Joshua Durden, the property owner went through the proper channels, filing a motion for summary judgment and securing a writ of possession.

Courts or local ordinances may view a tent as a “dwelling” or place of residence for the purposes of legal protections, especially in cases involving homeless individuals or unconventional living situations.* *The key factor is not whether it is a permanent or official building but whether it serves as a place where someone resides or occupies space.

This interpretation aligns with the legal principle that the law aims to protect individuals in their place of habitation, regardless of the structure’s form.* **7.

However, legal protections are not always contingent upon whether the property is officially recognized as a building.* *Courts often consider the purpose and use of the property.

This makes the matter entirely civil, as we have established possession of the property by setting up our tent.* *Florida Statute §82.01(2):* *‘Possession’ means the occupation of real property to the exclusion of others.* **9.

IN CONCLUSION…** *To summarize:* 1. *We qualify as squatters under Florida law.* 2. *Squatter removal is strictly a civil process, requiring the property owner to file an unlawful detainer action in court.* 3. *Law enforcement cannot remove us or force us to leave without a court-issued writ of possession.* 4. *The five-day notice posted by law enforcement is not a legally valid unlawful detainer action.* *Attempting to remove us without a writ of possession would violate Florida Statute §82.045(2) and constitutes an abuse of authority.

The Illegal Eviction at Chase Bank** In January 2025, a sergeant from the Miami-Dade Police Department (MDPD) visited our encampment, claiming to assist with our housing application through New Hope.

Despite presenting Officer Cheney with a detailed document explaining why the eviction was illegal under Florida law, she refused to even look at it. **Why the Eviction Was Illegal Under Florida Law** Under Florida law, our occupation of the Chase Bank property qualified as squatting, making it a civil matter, not a criminal one.

Florida Statute §82.03 and §83.21 mandate that property owners must pursue eviction through the courts, obtaining a writ of possession before law enforcement can intervene.

The police lacked this writ, violating Florida Statute §82.045(2), which explicitly prohibits law enforcement from removing occupants without a court order.

Florida Statute §82.045(4) requires a civil action for removal, which had not been filed.

The situation was further complicated by the nature of our dwelling—a tent—which, while unconventional, qualifies as a residence under legal interpretations that prioritize occupancy over structural form, as supported in *State v.

This case established that temporary structures used as residences are afforded certain legal protections.

Florida Statute §82.01(2) emphasizes that possession means exclusive occupation, which we had established.

Without a proper court process, the eviction was not only unlawful but a direct violation of our rights.

This eviction was an illegal self-help action, prohibited under Florida law, and compounded by false representations made by Officer Cheney, who refused to acknowledge the legal document presented to her.

This document detailed the statutory requirements and previous legal precedents, such as *Wireless Boutique, Inc. v.

Durden*, where proper legal channels were followed, and we complied accordingly.

The law mandates that local governments implement ordinances and programs to remove homeless individuals from public areas, with potential lawsuits from residents and the state for non-compliance.

While the law provides a $30 million budget for municipalities to address homelessness, local officials argue that this funding is insufficient to effectively tackle the issue. citeturn0news21 **Supreme Court Decision:** In June 2024, the U.S.

Supreme Court issued a ruling in *City of Grants Pass v.

Johnson*, determining that the Eighth Amendment's prohibition on "cruel and unusual punishment" does not prevent cities from enforcing criminal penalties against individuals experiencing homelessness for sleeping or camping in public spaces, even when no adequate shelter is available.

This decision effectively allows communities nationwide to fine, ticket, or arrest people living unsheltered. citeturn0search1 **Protections Against Worsening Conditions:** Currently, there are no specific provisions in Florida statutes or recent Supreme Court decisions that explicitly protect homeless individuals from being placed in worse situations as a result of the enforcement of these laws.

The recent legislative and judicial actions have, in fact, expanded the authority of local governments to penalize homelessness-related activities without mandating safeguards to prevent the deterioration of conditions for those affected.

Advocacy groups, including the American Civil Liberties Union (ACLU), have criticized these measures, arguing that criminalizing homelessness is counterproductive and inhumane.

They emphasize the need for policies that address the root causes of homelessness rather than punitive approaches that may exacerbate the challenges faced by this vulnerable population. citeturn0search3 In summary, recent legal developments in Florida and at the federal level have increased the criminalization of homelessness without incorporating explicit protections to ensure that enforcement does not worsen the circumstances of those experiencing homelessness. **Assistant (2025-02-21 20:51:03 UTC):** ### **Homeless Trust Complaint Letter - Version 24** **To Whom It May Concern at the Miami-Dade County Homeless Trust,** We are writing to file a formal complaint against New Hope C.O.R.P.S., an organization that is supposed to provide housing assistance but has instead engaged in a prolonged pattern of negligence, deception, and outright betrayal that has caused us significant harm.

Johnson* (2024)** - Ruled that cities **can** enforce camping bans and ticket or arrest individuals for sleeping in public spaces **even when no shelter is available**. - The ruling applies only to **public property** and does not override private property rights. ### **Private Property Protections:** - **Florida Law on Trespassing and Evictions:** - **Trespassing (§ 810.08, Florida Statutes)**: Law enforcement may only remove individuals from private property if the owner or a legally authorized representative files a trespass complaint. - **Self-Help Evictions Are Illegal (§ 83.67, Florida Statutes)**: Property owners must follow legal eviction procedures; they **cannot** forcibly remove occupants without due process. - **Unlawful Detainer (§ 82.03, Florida Statutes)**: If an individual has **established possession** of private property (even without a lease), the owner must go through the courts to regain possession. ### **Implications for Your Situation:** - Since you were residing on **private property** behind **Chase Bank**, the eviction should have followed **civil court procedures**, not a police-enforced removal. - Law enforcement cannot legally issue **public property** removal notices (**such as five-day notices to vacate**) on **private property**. - If law enforcement **or a third party** (such as a contractor hired by Home Depot) disposed of your property without a court order, this would be **illegal** and subject to legal action. ### **Next Steps:** - If you haven’t already, it may be beneficial to file a **formal complaint** against the officers and entities involved in the eviction. - Consider requesting **public records** from the police department to determine the justification (if any) they used for the removal.

Immediate Actions to Protect Yourself** Given your history with Calderon and law enforcement negligence, you may still face risks.
